Healthcare: Medicare for More vs. single-payer 8
Klein says Democrats no longer view healthcare as 'done' because Trump gutted Obamacare (mandate removed, subsidies cut) and individual-market premiums are spiraling.
causal🗣 host (Ezra Klein)conviction: highAffordable Care ActObamacareDonald Trump
So what: Rising individual-market premiums and subsidy loss pressure ACA-exchange enrollment; healthcare returns as a top legislative priority.
“the um insurance premiums in the individual markets are spiraling.” ▶ 23:54
Klein predicts the Democratic party is converging on a 'Medicare for more' or Medicare-choice synthesis, as AOC and El-Sayed walk away from abolishing private insurance.
prediction🗣 host (Ezra Klein)conviction: mediumMedicareAOCAbdul El-Sayedprivate insurance
So what: A public option/Medicare buy-in is the realistic policy path; it is a competitive threat to commercial insurers but less existential than single-payer.
“So I think you're going to end up with some kind of Medicare for more synthesis.” ▶ 24:31
Piker opposes a Medicare-for-America-style public option because private insurers will fight it as hard as single-payer, so Democrats 'might as well go all the way'.
contrarian🗣 Hasan Pikerconviction: mediumprivate insuranceMedicare for America
So what: Signals the left flank will resist incremental compromises, increasing the chance of gridlock on healthcare reform.
Piker argues healthcare is an inelastic service and insurers are an unnecessary middleman that boosts GDP and executive pay but drives up costs; he names Aetna and Blue Cross Blue Shield as widely despised.
thesis🗣 Hasan Pikerconviction: highAetnaBlue Cross Blue Shieldhealth insurers
So what: Insurer unpopularity is a durable political vulnerability for managed-care stocks; populist attacks on insurers likely intensify.
“they despise Aetna and and Blue Cross Blue Shield” ▶ 29:33
Klein counters that cost growth originates mainly with providers, which insurers pass through with a cut, so real Medicare for All savings require price controls on providers and doctors.
causal🗣 host (Ezra Klein)conviction: highhealth insurersprovidersMedicare for All
So what: Any serious cost-control push ultimately targets provider prices, a risk for hospital systems and physician groups, not just insurers.
“You do Medicare for all in a way that saves money, what you're doing is price controls on providers, right?” ▶ 29:59
Klein cites Vermont's failed single-payer attempt: folding employer and tax-deduction-hidden costs into visible taxes makes the price tag look larger to voters even if national spending falls.
framework🗣 host (Ezra Klein)conviction: highVermontsingle-payerBernie Sanders
So what: Explains why single-payer repeatedly stalls politically; employer-sponsored insurance is stickier than polling on Medicare for All suggests.
Klein cites a joint left-and-centrist poll (captioned as Media Sones Group and Searchlight) showing voters support Medicare for All but oppose losing private insurance and trust their insurer more than government.
data_point🗣 host (Ezra Klein)conviction: mediumSearchlightMedicare for Allprivate insurance

“Do you trust the government more than your private insurer? They say no.” ▶ 27:53

framework🗣 Hasan Pikerconviction: exploratoryTurkeymedical schoolhealthcare

Dwarkesh Podcast 1 episode(s) · 34 insights

Recursive self-improvement has started44 min34 insights2026-11-13
▶ Watch on YouTube
Solo video essay in which Dwarkesh Patel argues recursive self-improvement has begun (METR finds Anthropic's progress rate doubled; Claude leads 26% of Anthropic's R&D) and that because AI research is largely verifiable, automated researchers will match the best humans around 2028 (25% chance after 2032), triggering either a 'spark' of 3-6 years of progress in one year or a software-only singularity at 50/50 odds. He rebuts continual-learning, proxy, data and experiment-compute bottlenecks, with compute buildout as the long-run driver.
AI already accelerating frontier AI R&D · 5Why AI research is automatable (verifiability) · 6Timelines and takeoff probabilities · 6Mechanics of the speedup: faster, smarter, more · 4Compute, data and experiment bottlenecks · 8Generalization and real-world domains · 5
AI already accelerating frontier AI R&D 5
METR spent three months embedded inside Anthropic and concluded Anthropic's rate of AI progress on well-scoped tasks is 2x what it was a year ago, with a majority of the acceleration likely attributable to Anthropic's own use of AI for R&D.
data_point🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: highMETRAnthropic
So what: Independent third-party evidence that recursive self-improvement is measurably underway at a top lab; supports compute and frontier-lab demand theses.
≈ “Anthropic's rate of AI progress on well-scoped tasks is 2x what it was a year ago” paraphrase
OpenAI reported in September that the heaviest 10% of AI users among its researchers went from about $1,000 per day of internal coding-agent usage at the start of summer to over $7,000 per day by end of August.
data_point🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: highOpenAIcoding agents
So what: A 7x jump in per-researcher inference spend in ~3 months signals explosive internal inference demand; bullish for inference compute providers.
≈ “went from using about $1,000 a day of internal coding agents at the beginning of the summer to over $7,000 a day by the end of August” paraphrase
Anthropic reported in September that Claude now leads 26% of Anthropic's AI R&D work, up from under 1% as recently as February.
data_point🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: highAnthropicClaude
So what: Share of R&D led by AI is a leading indicator for automation of AI research; track subsequent disclosures for the trend.
AI math capability went from failing four-digit addition in 2023, to barely solving high-school competition problems in 2024, to IMO gold in 2025, to solving niche open problems this year, to OpenAI solving a Millennium Prize problem in 88 hours a month ago.
data_point🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: highOpenAIInternational Math OlympiadMillennium Prize
So what: Illustrates how fast AI crosses from worse-than-human to superhuman in verifiable domains; use as the template for expected AI-research automation pace.
≈ “OpenAI solved a Millennium Prize problem in 88 hours” paraphrase
Hundreds of important math results that top mathematicians had spent decades on are now being churned out by AIs as side quests to hype model releases, though progress skews to verification-laden work like finding counterexamples rather than new theory (a point Toby Ord makes).
thesis🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: highToby Ordmathematics
So what: Frontier labs are using solved open problems as marketing; capability in well-scoped verifiable tasks is far ahead of open-ended theory-building.
≈ “are being churned out as side quests to hype up future model releases” paraphrase
Why AI research is automatable (verifiability) 6
Grinding through well-scoped problems matters more for ML research than for math, because AI labs care about empirical gains in training efficiency rather than theory, and the last 15 years of deep learning progress required little new theory.
framework🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: highAI labsdeep learning
So what: Math-style AI capability transfers directly to economically valuable ML research, implying faster lab-level progress than math alone suggests.
≈ “But in AI research, the priorities are exactly reversed.” paraphrase
The transformer was a combination of known ideas (attention, residual networks, plus half a dozen tweaks) and could have been invented ~two years earlier; persistent combinatorial idea-matching of this kind is exactly what AIs are disproportionately good at, so Claude 7 could have invented it.
contrarian🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: mediumtransformerSholto DouglasChris OlahClaude 7AlexNet
So what: Even breakthrough-level innovation may be within reach of automated search, raising odds of rapid algorithmic progress.
≈ “is the kind of thing that Claude 7 could have invented” paraphrase
All of AI R&D falls into pretraining, post-training, and inference efficiency, each with strong verifiable proxies (held-out loss, RL compute/data/time, GPU utilization, KV cache per token, FLOPs, latency), so AI progress overall is easy to train against.
framework🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: highpretrainingpost-traininginference efficiency
So what: If every bucket of AI R&D is verifiable, it can be RL'd on; expect lab productivity gains in all three, especially inference efficiency, which lowers serving costs.
Research taste is partly trainable: an RL environment can make a model like GPT-7 predict whether an algorithmic improvement at 100M parameters will hold at 10B parameters, which is affordable at frontier-lab compute scale.
framework🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: mediumGPT-7nanoGPT speedrun
So what: Undercuts the main skeptic argument that human judgment is the irreplaceable bottleneck in AI research.
≈ “So even important parts of research taste are verifiable.” paraphrase
Anthropic has an internal benchmark called CoBench of about 500 real problems its researchers solved this year, which doubles as an RL environment letting models practice redoing the last three to six months of frontier progress.
data_point🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: mediumAnthropicCoBenchByteDanceEdgeBench
So what: Labs are building direct AI-R&D training loops; ByteDance's EdgeBench is cited as a benchmark for learning-on-the-fly tasks.
Proxies built before a paradigm shift capture the shift: the transformer was invented to beat existing translation benchmarks and reasoning models showed up on pre-existing coding and math benchmarks, and paradigm-agnostic outer-loop proxies (Millennium Prize problems solved, rediscovering general relativity from pre-1905 data) are easy to define.
causal🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: hightransformerreasoning modelsgeneral relativity
So what: Dwarkesh would be very surprised if the lack of verifiable proxies is what stops RSI; one fewer bear case for fast progress.
≈ “Proxies made before a paradigm leap seem perfectly capable of capturing large step changes in performance.” paraphrase
Timelines and takeoff probabilities 6
Dwarkesh's median year for automated researchers matching the very best human AI researchers is 2028, assuming labs go full steam ahead and are not slowed by themselves or government.
prediction🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: mediumAI labs
So what: A concrete public median from an influential voice; regulation/slowdown is the explicit swing factor to watch.
≈ “I'd guess 2028” paraphrase ▶ 8:27
Dwarkesh assigns at least a 25% chance that full automation of top (Ilya-level) AI researchers doesn't happen until after 2032, mainly due to continual learning possibly not being solved.
prediction🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: mediumIlya Sutskevercontinual learning
So what: Fat right tail; position sizing on AI-capex beneficiaries should account for a meaningful slow scenario.
≈ “at least a 25% chance that it doesn't happen until after 2032” paraphrase
A 2027 milestone is plausible: some well-calibrated researchers expect AIs to beat top humans at AI research next year, and Ajeya Cotra notes that plugging METR's 2x acceleration into a smooth takeoff model naively implies extreme superintelligence within a year.
prediction🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: mediumAjeya CotraMETR
So what: Markets should be prepared for a 2027 capability shock; Dwarkesh only avoids 2027 as median due to model uncertainty.
≈ “we should be ready for a world in which we get to this milestone next year” paraphrase
Since he expected superintelligence was at least the better part of a decade away earlier this year, Dwarkesh has updated in the last three months to expecting human-level AI researchers within a couple years and AIs as good as the best human experts in every field by 2030, plausibly as early as next year.
prediction🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: highsuperintelligence
So what: A prominent, previously skeptical commentator shifting sharply toward short timelines is a sentiment signal for AI-exposed assets.
≈ “by 2030, and plausibly as early as next year” paraphrase
Whether RSI yields a 'software-only singularity' (AI improvement outruns ideas getting harder to find, reaching chip-limited superintelligence in months) or a 'spark' (3-6 years of progress compressed into one year, then simmering until more compute is built) is a 50/50 coin toss.
framework🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: exploratorysoftware-only singularityRSI
So what: The spark scenario makes compute buildout the binding constraint after the burst, favoring chip, fab and datacenter suppliers; the singularity scenario is more discontinuous.
≈ “I'd give a 50% chance to the spark scenario and a 50% chance to the software-only singularity” paraphrase
Even the spark scenario implies a GPT-3.5-to-GPT-6 Astra-sized jump in 12 months, with Dwarkesh expecting something like a GPT-3.5 to GPT-6-sized jump in 2028 rather than an overnight singularity.
prediction🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: mediumGPT-3.5GPT-6 Astra
So what: Anchors the magnitude of near-term capability gains investors should underwrite.
≈ “That's a jump as big as GPT-3.5 to GPT-6 Astra in just 12 months.” paraphrase
Mechanics of the speedup: faster, smarter, more 4
Today's AIs already work at about 10x human speed and automated researchers would work around the clock, cramming roughly 50x more work per calendar week than a human researcher, though progress won't rise 50x because experiment compute is a bottleneck.
data_point🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: highautomated researchers
So what: Labor is about to become abundant relative to compute, shifting the bottleneck squarely onto compute supply.
≈ “each individual automated researcher will be cramming roughly 50 times more work every calendar week than a human researcher can do” paraphrase
Around 80% of a frontier lab's algorithmic progress is driven by its top 50 researchers, which is why Zuckerberg paid hundreds of millions to acquire talent; automated researchers all at top-human level would be a large speedup.
data_point🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: mediumZuckerbergfrontier labs
So what: Explains extreme AI talent pay; if top-researcher skill is copyable, the talent moat of incumbent labs erodes and compute becomes the moat.
≈ “around 80% of the algorithmic progress at a given frontier lab is driven by its 50 top researchers” paraphrase
AI research parallelizes like formalizing Fermat's Last Theorem (thousands of independently checkable modular subtasks) rather than like writing a novel, so swarms of agents can hill-climb pipeline components such as data filtering, RL environments and attention kernels in parallel.
framework🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: highClaudeFermat's Last Theorem
So what: Supports demand for massively parallel agent inference; returns to agent count are diminishing but at least a couple-fold.
≈ “AI research is closer to formalizing a theorem than it is to writing a novel” paraphrase
Ideas are already getting harder to find: AI companies must quadruple R&D compute and double their workforce every year just to maintain the same rate of progress.
data_point🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: highAI companies
So what: Implies structurally exploding compute budgets at labs even without RSI; core to the AI capex thesis.
≈ “AI companies have to quadruple their R&D compute and double their workforce every single year just to maintain the same rate of progress” paraphrase
Compute, data and experiment bottlenecks 8
Global AI compute has grown roughly 3x per year over the last half-decade, and post-spark AIs will be jaggedly superhuman at designing chips, laying out fabs and teleoperating robots to build fabs and datacenters, so progress continues via hardware scaling.
causal🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: highAI chipsfabsdatacentersrobots
So what: Hardware buildout is the long-run driver in the spark scenario; semis, fab equipment, datacenter and robotics are the beneficiaries.
≈ “global AI compute has been growing roughly 3x a year for the last half-decade” paraphrase
Frontier labs have been more than quadrupling compute yearly; if the trend continues, by end of 2027 OpenAI could give 10,000 agents each enough compute for a GPT-3-sized training run every day, versus the original 2017 transformer that took three days on eight P100s (about $50 to rent today).
data_point🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: mediumOpenAIGPT-3P100transformer
So what: Experiment-compute starvation is unlikely to stall RSI, which keeps the fast-takeoff scenario live.
“OpenAI could almost give 10,000 agents” ▶ 40:44
Labs today waste compute to save scarce researcher time (top researchers paid hundreds of millions a year), so automated researchers can trade labor for compute via many small ablations, reading every RL rollout, and spending subjective decades designing pooled experiments.
contrarian🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: mediumfrontier labs
So what: Today's compute usage understates how much more productive each GPU can become; compute efficiency gains could be large.
≈ “researcher time is extremely scarce relative to compute” paraphrase
Better science of small-scale experiments can stretch compute: GPT-4's final loss was predicted with models trained on 10,000x less compute, and muP predicts optimal hyperparameters from 100x smaller models; automated researchers could discover more such scaling phenomena.
causal🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: mediumGPT-4muPscaling laws
So what: More extrapolation tools reduce compute needed per research insight.
≈ “The gods above must work from the same principles as the ants below.” paraphrase
Research with Princeton student Jerry Han found that over the last six years more than three times as much pretraining algorithmic progress came from data improvements as from model improvements, and data work is normal verifiable ML research AIs can do.
data_point🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: highJerry HanPrincetonpretraining
So what: Data curation/synthesis is the key lever and is itself automatable, weakening the 'data wall' bear case.
≈ “more than three times the algorithmic progress has come from data improvements than model improvements” paraphrase
DeepSeek's latest post-training paper says changes to the RL algorithm mattered little and systematic improvements in scale, diversity and verifiability of synthesized data and environments account for essentially all observed gains, with environment construction increasingly automated.
data_point🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: highDeepSeek
So what: Competitive advantage shifts to automated data/environment pipelines; RL environment builders are a growth area.
≈ “account for essentially all of the observed gains” paraphrase
Pretraining is far from its limits: AI lab Magic pretrained a model matching leading open-source base models like DeepSeek V4 Pro for half the compute of the original GPT-3, roughly $500,000 today, and orders of magnitude further improvement seem plausible.
data_point🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: mediumMagicDeepSeek V4 ProGPT-3
So what: Rapidly falling training cost for frontier-ish base models compresses moats around base-model capability and pressures model pricing.
“We are far from the limits of pretraining.” ▶ 31:38
During RSI, labs likely won't run three-month pretraining runs across multiple campuses because a much smaller model started a week later would make a giant run obsolete before it finished; smaller models also tighten the RSI loop and support larger researcher populations.
prediction🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: exploratorypretrainingdatacenters
So what: Could change the shape of compute demand from giant single training runs to many smaller runs and inference; relevant for multi-campus datacenter assumptions.
≈ “It's similar to how if you launch a spaceship to Alpha Centauri with today's technology” paraphrase
Generalization and real-world domains 5
Scaling RL won't bake every answer into weights but will give AIs general skills to ramp quickly in hard-to-verify domains, with automated engineers building tens of thousands of times more, larger, open-ended RL environments such as simulated economies of millions of AIs and historical backtests of wars, elections and quarterly earnings.
thesis🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: mediumRL environments
So what: Signals AI moving into business, trading, law and politics; the RL-environment buildout is a major spend category.
“and quarterly earnings that has ever happened” ▶ 20:03
A post-spark AI would plausibly be better than Nvidia at designing next-gen AI accelerators, out-earn Citadel and Jane Street with equal market access, and beat humanity at finding a room-temperature superconductor given a remote materials lab.
prediction🗣 Dwarkesh Patelconviction: exploratoryNvidiaCitadelJane Street

Is Ethereum's $240 Billion Market Cap Justified With Only $6 Million in Monthly Fees?40 min33 insights2026-11-13
▶ Watch on YouTube
Arca CIO Jeff Dorman argues ETH (~$246B) and SOL (~$50B) are overwhelmingly memetic value: Ethereum earned ~$6M and Solana ~$1.7M in 30-day revenue, so L1 tokens are overvalued while revenue-rich apps like Hyperliquid and Pump.fun (3x P/S) are undervalued. He calls Vitalik's ETH store-of-value pivot misguided and says L1s must own DEX/stablecoin revenue (Hyperliquid's 'holy trinity') or tax apps.
Three-bucket token valuation framework · 4ETH and SOL fundamentals vs market cap · 6Application tokens capture the value · 6Hyperliquid 'holy trinity' L1 model · 4Store-of-value narrative critique · 6Where crypto growth actually is · 5Show me the number: subsidize-then-monetize · 2
Three-bucket token valuation framework 4
Jeff Dorman argues any asset has only three sources of value: financial (cash flows a multiple can be applied to), utility (something you must hold or use), and social/memetic value (cool factor, cult identity).
framework🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: highGraham and DoddWarren Buffett
So what: Use a sum-of-the-parts across financial, utility and memetic value to size how much of a token's price has no fundamental floor.
“there's really only three types of value that it can have” ▶ 5:28
Dorman estimates roughly 75-80% of the value of smart-contract L1s like Ethereum and Solana sits in social/memetic value, which is why they trade so violently when sentiment oscillates: there is little financial or utility floor beneath them.
thesis🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: highEthereumSalanaArca
So what: Expect L1 tokens to have deep drawdowns when narrative fades; size positions assuming the memetic premium can evaporate.
“it it's probably closer to 75 to 80% of the value of these chains is purely in um that social value camp” ▶ 10:09
Dorman cites a January 18, 2025 Joe Lubin tweet saying Bitcoin is 80% memecoin / 18% store of value / 2% other and ETH is 65% memecoin / 25% utility / 10% store of value, as evidence even Ethereum insiders concede value is mostly memetic.
data_point🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: mediumJoe LubenBitcoinETH
So what: Even Consensys founder framing ETH as majority memecoin undercuts fundamental bull cases for ETH.
“ETH is 65% memecoin, 25% utility coin, and 10% store value” ▶ 7:49
Dorman says that having to repeatedly justify what an 11-year-old asset like ETH is signals that nobody understands it and supporters are grasping at straws to create value.
contrarian🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: highETHVitalic Bdderin
So what: Treat Vitalik's repositioning of ETH as store of value as a narrative-maintenance signal rather than new fundamentals.
“if you have to continuously justify what an asset is, then clearly nobody understands what it is” ▶ 5:09
ETH and SOL fundamentals vs market cap 6
Per DeFi Llama, Ethereum generated about $6 million of revenue in the last 30 days, barely in the top 50 protocols; even doubling it to ~$10M/month ($120M/yr) at a generous 20x multiple yields only a couple of billion dollars of financial value.
data_point🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: highEthereumDeFi Lama
So what: Ethereum's ~$240B valuation is not supported by protocol revenue; fee-based valuation implies a tiny fraction of market cap.
“It did $6 million of revenue in the last 30 days.” ▶ 10:56
Dorman sizes ETH's utility value at roughly $40B: about 200 million Ethereum wallets holding ~0.1 ETH on average equals ~20 million ETH held for gas at ~$2,000, leaving roughly $200B of a ~$246B market cap as pure social value.
data_point🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: highETHEthereum
So what: Roughly 80% of ETH's price is unexplained by revenue or gas demand, a large downside gap if narrative breaks.
“That leaves 200 billion left over of just social value or opium.” ▶ 12:33
Solana generated only about $1.7 million of fees in the last 30 days and is not in DeFi Llama's top 100 by revenue; even generously annualized at ~$60M it supports at most $5-10B of value versus a roughly $50B market cap.
data_point🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: highSalanaDeFi Lama
So what: SOL screens even more overvalued than ETH on revenue; skeptical of 'win network first, monetize later' narratives.
“the Salana network has generated 1.7 million of fees” ▶ 13:05
Dorman estimates Solana's gas-utility value at only ~$200M: about 100 million SOL wallets holding ~0.02 SOL on average (~2 million SOL) at ~$100 per SOL.
data_point🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: mediumSalanasoul
So what: Utility demand for SOL as gas is negligible relative to market cap, so price rests on speculation.
Dorman dismisses valuing Ethereum's role securing layer-2s because with countless chains and virtually no cost to move between them, blockspace is a perfect substitute in economic terms.
contrarian🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: highEthereumlayer twos
So what: L2 security as an ETH value-accrual argument is weak if blockspace is commoditized; watch whether L2s ever pay meaningful rent.
“as an economist, you would call that perfect substitutes” ▶ 11:45

Application tokens capture the value 6

Hyperliquid 'holy trinity' L1 model 4
Dorman considers Hyperliquid the only successful L1 model because it owns the 'holy trinity': the L1 for distribution, a native DEX generating fees, and stablecoin revenue, since even Circle pays almost all USDC revenue on Hyperliquid to Hyperliquid as distribution.
thesis🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: highHyperlidCircleUSDC
So what: Vertically integrated chains that capture DEX and stablecoin economics deserve premium valuations over pure hosting L1s.
“You've got the holy trinity.” ▶ 27:00
Ethereum is the opposite of Hyperliquid: DEX revenue goes to Uniswap and Aero and stablecoin fees go to Circle and Tether, so ETH earns little for merely hosting when a hundred other chains can host as easily.
causal🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: highEthereumUniswapArrowCircleTether
So what: Value leaks from Ethereum to apps built on it; prefer the apps over the base asset.
“The DEX revenues don't go to Ethereum.” ▶ 27:15
Dorman observes a convergence race: stablecoin issuers (Tether, Circle) are building their own chains and DEXes, DEXes are building stablecoins and chains, while most chains do nothing but should be building native stablecoins and DEXes.
framework🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: mediumTetherCircle
So what: Watch for vertical integration moves; L1s without native DEX/stablecoin risk disintermediation by issuer chains.
“What are the chains doing? Most of them doing nothing.” ▶ 31:41
Dorman's prescription for Ethereum: since it cannot roll back to own its stablecoin and DEX, it must tax applications for running on it and see how many stay versus migrate to cheaper chains; he declined to name a rate but said it would need to be high.
prediction🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: mediumEthereum
So what: Any Ethereum move toward charging apps (or native rollups) would be a key catalyst for ETH value accrual; watch app migration if it happens.
“tax them and prove that you're you're worthwhile” ▶ 32:38
Store-of-value narrative critique 6
Vitalik Buterin tweeted that ETH should prioritize being a store of value over a fee-burning token, arguing the burn was mostly crypto-internal (e.g. the Otherdeed NFT sale burned $180M in a day) and that optimizing for fees to justify a ~$270B market cap would make Ethereum function like a major corporation around L'Oreal's size.
data_point🗣 Laura Shinconviction: mediumVitalic BdderinETHEIP1559
So what: Ethereum leadership is de-emphasizing fee-burn value accrual, weakening the 'ultrasound money' cash-flow thesis.
“Optimizing for ETH as a fee burning token would demand Ethereum and ETH function in ways that are very hard to make compatible with decentralization and capture resistance.” ▶ 1:29
Laura Shin notes EIP-1559 (2021) tied network activity to ETH burn, but the spring 2024 Dencun upgrade decoupled activity from fee burning.
causal🗣 Laura Shinconviction: highEIP1559DenuneEthereum
So what: Post-Dencun, higher Ethereum usage no longer mechanically supports ETH price via burn.
Dorman calls Vitalik's store-of-value positioning really misguided: Bitcoin was a one-in-a-billion 'immaculate conception' and comping other tokens to it is like saying the Zimbabwe dollar will be a store of value because the US dollar became one.
contrarian🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: highBitcoinETH
So what: Discount store-of-value narratives for non-BTC tokens; only Bitcoin has earned that status.
“Bitcoin was effectively like an immaculate uh conception” ▶ 28:03
For ETH to be a store of value or medium of exchange, Dorman argues one must believe every other blockchain disappears and the whole world runs on Ethereum, for which there is no evidence given multi-chain RWA tokenization on Canton, Avalanche and others.
thesis🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: highETHCantonAvalanche
So what: Multi-chain fragmentation undermines any single L1 money premium.
Using a blockchains-as-nations analogy (from an Arca blog by Nick Ho, Feb 2022), Dorman argues a chain's currency only appreciates if it taxes the businesses on it, like a nation's tax receipts; Ethereum and other L1s don't tax applications.
framework🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: highNick HoesArcaEthereum
So what: Assess L1s on their ability to extract rent from apps, analogous to tax receipts.
“They don't tax the applications, right?” ▶ 19:35

contrarian🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: mediumVitalicEthereum Foundation

So what: Tokenized equities trading on crypto venues is a key growth vector; platforms enabling it benefit.
“even Binance and Hyperliquid are effectively just stock trading platforms now” ▶ 24:21
DeFi has grown from under 1% of crypto transaction volume versus centralized exchanges like Coinbase and Binance to about 25% of all volume, and Dorman expects that share to keep growing.
data_point🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: highDeFiCoinbaseBinance
So what: Secular share shift from CEXs to DeFi favors DEX and DeFi tokens.
“Now DeFi transactions are 25% of all volume” ▶ 24:52
Dorman concedes ETH is the laziest, most generic way to get exposure to all three growth areas (stablecoins, RWA, DeFi), while Bitcoin gives exposure to none and is an anomaly disconnected from blockchain growth.
framework🗣 Jeff Dormanconviction: mediumEthereumBitcoin
So what: Being 'long crypto' via BTC isn't a bet on blockchain adoption; ETH is beta, apps are alpha.
“Bitcoin gives you exposure to none of those things” ▶ 25:24

a16z Podcast 1 episode(s) · 32 insights

Cyberattacks Used to Take Months. AI Can Do It in Minutes.46 min32 insights2026-11-13
▶ Watch on YouTube
Wiz's Ammy argues 'Black October' marks the arrival of real AI attacks: frontier and fast-catching-up open source models now find and exploit vulnerabilities (e.g. PHP-to-shell) and run full attacks in ~20 minutes, while patching lags. The answer is continuous AI self-attack (red agent) focused on external exposure, cost-efficient multi-model pipelines, secure design over budget, and dropping non-security work.
Black October: AI attacks go live · 5PHP-to-shell and the patching gap · 6Multi-model pipelines and AI security unit economics · 4Red agent: continuous self-attack beats code scanning · 4Startups and AI platforms as prime targets · 3Security budgets, design and obsolete tools · 7Next 2-3 years: back to the wild west · 3
Black October: AI attacks go live 5
Wiz calls October 2026 'Black October', a black-swan month with an unprecedented number and severity of vulnerabilities plus the rise of real AI-driven attacks on companies, startups and even refrigerators.
thesis🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highWizBlack OctoberPHP
So what: Security incident volume has stepped up structurally; expect demand pull for AI-native security vendors and rising breach headlines.
“we've never seen never ever seen so many um security incidents happen in a single month” ▶ 1:36
The path to Black October ran through two warning moments: the 'Mitos' model showing AI could find vulnerabilities, and an accidental 'hacking face' (likely Hugging Face) incident involving OpenAI; in October those theories materialized at once.
causal🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: mediumMitosOpenAIhacking face
So what: Frontier-model capability releases are now leading indicators for security incident waves.
Frontier models are now good enough to find AND exploit complex vulnerabilities, and an attack chain that took the best human attackers weeks or months now runs in around 20 minutes.
data_point🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highAI models
So what: Detection-and-response windows measured in hours are obsolete; value shifts to automated, minute-scale response.
“basically run an entire attack that usually took for the best attackers weeks or months in again around 20 minutes” ▶ 4:59
Open source models are catching up on offensive capability far faster than expected: Wiz thinks frontier advantage in vulnerability finding is no more than ~3 months, versus a16z hearing 6-9 months and an earlier hope of a year.
data_point🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highopen source modelsWiza16z
So what: Commoditized offensive AI means unsophisticated attackers get elite capability; the defender's lead from frontier-model access is thin.
“the hope was that open source models are a year behind but they're not” ▶ 42:01
AI is arguably the only domain where AI is already better than humans: AI attacks are prevalent, easy and better than any human, e.g. a kernel-driver zero-day attack that should be rare is now happening daily.
contrarian🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highAI attacksLinux
So what: Offense has superhuman capability before defense does; frames a multi-year asymmetric threat cycle favoring security spend.
“this is almost the only area in humankind right now that AI is better than humans” ▶ 42:23
PHP-to-shell and the patching gap 6
The PHP-to-shell vulnerability, discovered independently by Wiz and Califf, is probably the most severe vulnerability ever found by AI, and almost 100% of Wiz customers had some PHP running.
data_point🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highPHPWizCaliff
So what: Single AI-found bugs can now hit near-universal attack surfaces; watch for remediation spend spikes.
After the PHP disclosure about 50% of affected Wiz customers patched within the first 2-3 days, but because PHP is ~70% of the internet and older versions get no patch, this single vulnerability will take years to resolve.
data_point🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highPHPWiz
So what: Long tail of unpatchable legacy exposure creates persistent breach risk and sustained demand for exposure management.
“is going to take years okay until it's resolved” ▶ 8:17
Time to weaponize a patch into an exploit has collapsed while time to patch has not fallen (it has risen over 20-25 years); information often leaks before the CVE or patch, as happened with PHP despite months of coordinated work.
causal🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highPHPCVE
So what: The exploit-vs-patch asymmetry is widening; vendors that shrink time-to-remediate capture value.
Linux kernel fixes can take weeks or months to reach all distros, so even companies that patch quickly internally are blocked; the industry is 'not ready' for minute-scale AI exploitation.
causal🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highLinux
So what: Upstream supply-chain latency in patching is a structural weakness that no single buyer can fix.
“the world is not ready. The world is not ready for this” ▶ 10:18
Ammy sees no clear path to automated patching for legacy companies, but new startups should design for auto-patch with a fully agentic pipeline: automated dev from ticket, automated testing, CI/CD and DevOps.
framework🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: mediumCI/CDDevOps
So what: Greenfield companies with agentic SDLC gain a security advantage; tooling for automated remediation is a startup opportunity.
“for new companies, you should design your system to be able to auto patch” ▶ 10:54
Vulnerability discovery is like Bitcoin mining: each new model uncovers more bugs, so multiple teams will increasingly find the same vulnerabilities at once, as Wiz sees with multiple reports of a critical bug ahead of its ZDC zero-day competition.
framework🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: mediumBitcoinWizZDC
So what: Each frontier model release likely triggers a new wave of disclosures; time security budgets and positions to model launches.
“I look at it like Bitcoin mining” ▶ 12:21
Multi-model pipelines and AI security unit economics 4
Wiz runs Atlas, an internal vulnerability-detection harness, across many models from OpenAI, Google and others because each model and each cyber-tuned version behaves differently in capability, speed and cost.
anecdote🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highWizAtlasOpenAIGoogleDeepMind
So what: Independent security vendors stay model-agnostic even inside Google; frontier labs are suppliers, not sole winners.
The key engineering challenge for AI companies at scale is cost: a scalable service requires intelligent pipelines that route simple tasks to cheap models and hard ones to expensive models, otherwise you lose money on sales.
thesis🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highAI pipelines
So what: Gross margin of AI-native software depends on model routing; evaluate startups on pipeline efficiency, not just model access.
“otherwise you're you're you're going to lose money on on your sales” ▶ 16:12
Wiz's blue agent escalates through deterministic detection, then a cheap model, then a more advanced model, and only invokes the most advanced model to confirm maliciousness before alerting the customer.
framework🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highWizblue agent
So what: Tiered-inference architecture is a template for cost-efficient AI SOC products.
Brute-force code scanning with frontier models is very expensive: a company that scanned all its code after the Mitos moment in April might have wasted $5 million and must redo it with each new model like Mitos 1.2, and some customers now spend tens of millions on code scanning.
data_point🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highMitosWiz
So what: Huge new spend is flowing to AI code scanning, but much is inefficient; vendors that optimize continuous scanning budgets win.
“some customers are spending now tens of millions of dollars on like scanning code” ▶ 18:21
Red agent: continuous self-attack beats code scanning 4
Defenses must become dynamic because every new model raises the attacker 'watermark': an application secure yesterday may not be secure today, so companies must continuously attack themselves with the latest models.
thesis🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highred agentWiz
So what: Continuous AI red-teaming / attack-surface testing is becoming a must-have budget line.
“whatever was considered secure last week, if every AI model can get into the door, it's not secure anymore” ▶ 23:53
Wiz's red-side pipeline starts with deterministic exposure analysis, then an exposure agent spending minutes per endpoint on unauthenticated internet-facing surfaces, then deep pentests taking up to 10 hours on critical applications, with unfixable findings handed to the blue agent for detections.
framework🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highWizred agentblue agent
So what: Integrated red/blue platforms create cross-sell and lock-in; tiered compute budgeting is the moat.
The majority of Wiz customers already use the red agent, approaching full coverage, making it one of the fastest growing features Wiz has ever released.
data_point🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highWizred agent
So what: Strong adoption signal for AI offensive-security products inside Google's Wiz; read-through for competing exposure-management vendors.
“it's really one of the fastest growing features that we've ever released” ▶ 27:51
Ammy argues the Mitos PR pushed the market toward code scanning, which is not the most efficient spend; scanning unauthenticated external exposure continuously matters more, as shown by a recent breach from a key hardcoded in a mobile app.
contrarian🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highMitosWiz
So what: Budget may rotate from AI code-scanning toward external attack-surface management; pick vendors accordingly.
“I actually don't think the scanning code is the most efficient thing you can do” ▶ 28:06
Startups and AI platforms as prime targets 3
AI development platforms are being specifically targeted because they hold AI keys: Wiz's blog initially listed nine such companies attacked in October and the list has grown to 15.
data_point🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highWizAI development platforms
So what: AI dev-tool startups carry outsized breach risk; diligence their security posture and key management.
Startups holding AI keys are now attackers' key target for LLM hijacking, and a whole attack from entry to stealing all customers' secrets and AI keys takes about 20 minutes, so startups effectively need a 24/7 SOC.
thesis🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highstartupsLLM
So what: Demand for managed/automated SOC for small companies; founders should cap API key spend limits immediately.
“Startups are actually their key target now for for LLM checking” ▶ 31:29
a16z's Zayn and Joel advised portfolio founders during Black October to spend more on security and consider delaying releases, while Zayn argues the new model is running security in parallel via blue and red agents rather than delaying ships.
thesis🗣 Zayn (a16z)conviction: mediuma16zZaynJoel
So what: VC-level guidance signals portfolio-wide security spend increases.
Security budgets, design and obsolete tools 7
A CISO told a16z he expects his security budget to rise 80% next year, at the high end of expected increases.
data_point🗣 host (a16z)conviction: mediumCISOa16z

Ammy argues budget alone won't solve AI-era risk; it is about secure design: sandboxes, isolation, network/process/image lockdown, and assuming container escapes since Linux zero-days are constant.
contrarian🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highKubernetesLinux
So what: Favors isolation/sandboxing and secure-architecture vendors over point tools.

Most legacy security tools are no longer relevant, and classic CSPM configuration-check lists are low value compared with understanding where the attacker will come from.
contrarian🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highCSPM
So what: Legacy security vendors and checklist-style CSPM face displacement by AI-native platforms.

Zayn sees CISOs rebooting their vendor stacks toward AI-native tools in a step change similar to the cloud transition.
prediction🗣 Zayn (a16z)conviction: mediumCISO
So what: Expect a vendor replacement cycle benefiting AI-native security startups.
Most of what security teams do (compliance, reports) is not security; teams should focus solely on finding critical risk, mitigating or adding detections, and response readiness.
contrarian🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: highsecurity teams

Customers now expect Wiz to go beyond prioritized findings: trace back to code, generate the fix and pull request, and run full investigations and forensics; the next step is automated response, which Ammy calls scary but unavoidable.
prediction🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: mediumWiz
So what: Security platforms are expanding into remediation and autonomous response, enlarging TAM.
Legacy enterprises with thousands of developers and unclear resource ownership will take time to reach security automation because organizational knowledge is undocumented, while new companies can build fully automated security from scratch.
causal🗣 Ammy (Wiz)conviction: mediumenterprises

Next 2-3 years: back to the wild west 3
Zayn predicts security is going back to the 1990s-2000s with constant unauthenticated full RCEs, frequent breaches, and possibly internet worms like the Windows XP era when systems were infected before they could be patched.
prediction🗣 Zayn (a16z)conviction: mediumWindows XP

Yardeni Research · Ed Yardeni 1 episode(s) · 32 insights

The Hire-No-Fire AI Economy34 min32 insights2026-11-13
▶ Watch on YouTube
Ed Yardeni and colleague Elias argue the post-midterm Nasdaq selloff is a buying opportunity in an earnings-led bull market with a compressed ~17x forward P/E, a 4-5% 10-year yield as the normal range, and a 7,900 S&P 500 year-end target. The labor market is a 'hire, no fire' economy (~5M monthly hires offset by separations) with no sign yet of AI job losses, and AI capex faces speed bumps rather than roadblocks.

Bond yields: 4-5% is the new normal 4
Yardeni argues a 4-5% 10-year Treasury yield is the normal range, as in the four years before the Great Financial Crisis, and that the low yields between the GFC and the pandemic were the abnormal period, so 'higher for longer' does not imply yields must come down.
framework🗣 Ed Yardeniconviction: high10-year Treasurybond market
So what: Don't position for a return to sub-4% yields; anchor valuation models to a 4-5% range.
“The the normal is 4 to 5%.” ▶ 4:12
When the 10-year yield hit 5.35%, buyers started appearing around a 5% 'handle', leading Yardeni to think the worst of the bond selloff was over; he expects yields near 5% at end of this year and next, with buyers stepping in around 5.01%.
prediction🗣 Ed Yardeniconviction: medium10-year Treasury

'Hire-no-fire' labor market and AI jobs impact 7
The October employment report was strong and broadening: unemployment fell even as labor force participation rose, and average hourly earnings rose 0.4% month over month, with the prior two months revised higher.
data_point🗣 Elias Grienrogconviction: highemployment reportaverage hourly earnings
So what: Labor data does not support recession fears; supports consumer spending and earnings.
Yardeni and Elias reject the 'no hire, no fire' label: JOLTS shows hires of about 5 million per month (5.4 million in September, not annualized) offset by separations of 5.3 million, so low net hiring masks heavy churn; they rename it the 'hire, no fire' economy because layoffs are just not happening.
contrarian🗣 Ed Yardeniconviction: highJOLTS
So what: Low net payroll growth is balance at full employment (~4-4.5% unemployment), not stagnation.
“the net net has been looks like no hires, no fires, but that's not really what's what what's happening at all.” ▶ 19:33
There is no evidence yet of AI causing widespread job losses; manufacturing added about 19,000 jobs in October, including roughly 7,000 in electronic and computer products and 7,900 in non-residential trade contractors, which Elias attributes to the data center buildout.
causal🗣 Elias Grienrogconviction: highAIdata centersmanufacturing
So what: The AI buildout is a net job creator for now; watch manufacturing and specialty trade employment as capex proxies.
“clearly no no evidence yet of of AI causing any sort of widespread job losses.” ▶ 20:53
Professional and business services employment surged in October, the strongest monthly increase since 2022 according to Elias.
data_point🗣 Elias Grienrogconviction: mediumprofessional and business services
So what: Counters the narrative that AI is already hollowing out white-collar hiring.
The private-sector employment diffusion index shows 60.4% of industries adding jobs on a three-month average, the highest since 2023 and in a solid uptrend, and the manufacturing diffusion index and ISM manufacturing also show signs of life.
data_point🗣 Elias Grienrogconviction: mediumdiffusion indexISMmanufacturing
So what: Broadening job growth reduces dependence on a few sectors and supports a broader equity rally.
Latest productivity growth was 3.4% quarter over quarter with modest unit labor costs, suggesting labor is not a source of inflation; Elias expects productivity growth to reach 4-5% in the 'Roaring 2020s' environment, while Yardeni is more conservative at 3.5-4%.
prediction🗣 Elias Grienrogconviction: mediumproductivityunit labor costsRoaring 2020s

causal🗣 Ed Yardeniconviction: mediumbaby boomersretail sales

AI capex: speed bumps, not roadblocks 7
Elias tracks three constraints on the AI buildout, political/social, financial and physical, and argues the political and financial ones now in focus will slow but not stop the buildout: speed bumps rather than roadblocks.
framework🗣 Elias Grienrogconviction: highAIdata centers
So what: Expect a slower, not collapsing, AI capex cycle; avoid pricing an AI bust.
“they will put speed bumps sort of on the path of the AI investment boom, but they're not going to be roadblocks” ▶ 22:41
Yardeni argues physical constraints (construction workers, land, permitting) would have slowed the AI buildout anyway, so political and fixed-income market pushback simply aligns spending with available resources, letting air out of the AI bubble without bursting it.
contrarian🗣 Ed Yardeniconviction: highAIfixed income markets

Robinson Erhardt 1 episode(s) · 31 insights

Professor Jiang: They WANT the AI Crash | On Altman, Zuckerberg, & Silicon Valley118 min31 insights2026-11-14
▶ Watch on YouTube
Jiang argues the AI boom is a liquidity-fueled bubble built on circular OpenAI-Oracle-Nvidia financing and an unrealistic $10T payday, that Iran-war strain on Saudi and Japanese capital plus a possibly weak Anthropic IPO will pop it, and that AI firms want a government bailout leading to nationalized data centers and a digital-ID surveillance state. Much of the episode is an occult/Freemasonry framing of AI elites that the host Robinson Erhardt rejects; investable content centers on bubble mechanics, model commoditization via DeepSeek, critical-mineral constraints, and stablecoin legislation (Genius Act, Clarity Act).

AI bubble mechanics: circular financing and the $10T payday 7
Jiang argues the AI buildout rests on an assumed $10 trillion payday that justifies roughly $1 trillion of data-center capex, and that once investors recognize the payday is not coming the chain collapses like dominoes.
thesis🗣 Jiang (guest)conviction: highOpenAIAI data centers
So what: Frames AI capex as reflexive: watch for any signal that end-revenue expectations are being cut, since the whole stack is priced off them.
“the problem is like at the moment people recognize that this $10 trillion payout is not coming. The entire thing collapses, right? It's like a domino effect.” ▶ 20:39
Jiang describes a circular financing chain in which OpenAI commits to roughly $1 trillion of compute from Oracle, Oracle needs about $50 billion of Nvidia chips it cannot pay for, and Nvidia lends Oracle the money to buy its own chips.
framework🗣 Jiang (guest)conviction: highOpenAIOracleNvidia
So what: Vendor financing between Nvidia, Oracle and OpenAI is the pressure point; scrutinize counterparty exposure and receivables in ORCL/NVDA filings.
“Oracle says to Nvidia, I need to purchase about $50 billion of chips from you in order to build out my data centers. Can I borrow that from you? And Nvidia is sure.” ▶ 19:47
Jiang says AI companies currently make no money because startup costs were too high and competition is too fierce, and the Silicon Valley playbook is to burn capital until the product saturates daily life and pricing power follows.
thesis🗣 Jiang (guest)conviction: highSilicon ValleyAI companies
So what: Implies AI lab economics depend on achieving lock-in; margin expansion is a bet on saturation, not current unit economics.
“you throw money at a problem until you monopolize the market and you can charge whatever price you want.” ▶ 14:10
Jiang calls the AI build-out 'definitely a bubble' and a Ponzi-like scheme in which the more foreign investors commit, the more committed they become to sustaining it.
thesis🗣 Jiang (guest)conviction: highAI bubble
So what: Bearish stance on AI-linked equities; position sizing around momentum-driven capex names warrants caution.
“it's definitely a bubble.” ▶ 20:56
Jiang claims roughly 50% of US GDP growth is attributable to data-center build-outs, making an AI bust a subprime-scale threat to the US economy.
data_point🗣 Jiang (guest)conviction: mediumUS economydata centers
So what: If capex is that large a share of growth, an AI capex slowdown is a macro (rates, GDP) event, not just a sector event.
“50% of GDP growth is due to data center buildouts” ▶ 23:03
Jiang says Anthropic's IPO, which he says is scheduled for November at a roughly $2 trillion valuation, is a key 'limits test': a weak investor response and falling valuation could trigger collapse of the broader AI bubble.
prediction🗣 Jiang (guest)conviction: highAnthropicIPO
So what: Treat the Anthropic IPO reception as a sentiment bellwether for the entire AI complex.
“one limits test will be the anthropic IPO which is scheduled for November.” ▶ 21:02

framework🗣 Albert Louconviction: highrailroadsaviationautomobileinternetAI

framework🗣 Albert Louconviction: highNASDAQdot-com
So what: Size equity exposure to survive a decade-long recovery, not just a correction.
Lou calls AI a mega trend rather than a trend, meaning money will be made over the next couple of decades through iterative innovation, as with the internet — so there is no need to chase early speculative gains.
thesis🗣 Albert Louconviction: highAIinternet
So what: Patience is rewarded: waiting for clarity on winners sacrifices little in a multi-decade trend.
“AI it's not it's not just a trend. I would call it a mega trend.” ▶ 9:12

data_point🗣 Albert Louconviction: highAmazon.comdot-com

Broken 60/40 and the cost of cash 5
The multi-decade era of falling interest rates is over, so bonds no longer deliver the steady gains plus capital appreciation they once did.
thesis🗣 Albert Louconviction: highbondsinterest rates
So what: Do not rely on duration for returns or as an automatic hedge.
With stocks and bonds moving together and both expensive at once, blended 60/40 or 70/30 portfolios no longer behave as before, and buying both when expensive risks a lost decade or more.
causal🗣 Albert Louconviction: high60/40stocksbonds
So what: Traditional balanced portfolios need a third diversifier (e.g., real assets) to avoid a lost decade.
“if you buy when when both stocks and bonds are expensive you could have like a you know lost decade or more” ▶ 13:44
Holding cash is fine as long as investors recognize its rising cost: with essentials inflating 5–6% a year versus cash at ~3% or Treasuries around 4.5%, the gap is the price of insurance.
framework🗣 Albert Louconviction: highcashTreasuriesinflationFed
So what: Cash is an insurance premium with negative real carry; size it deliberately.
“the difference between that and the inflation is is the cost of your insurance” ▶ 19:18
Lou says inflation is running far above the Fed's 2% target even by the Fed's own measure, that the economy has been inflationary for his whole lifetime apart from brief deflationary shocks, and that the stated target will probably go up.
prediction🗣 Albert Louconviction: highFedinflation

thesis🗣 Albert Louconviction: highbondsduration

The Great Simplification · Nate Hagens 1 episode(s) · 25 insights

Rules of the Downslope (Part 1) | Frankly 16630 min25 insights2026-11-13
▶ Watch on YouTube
Solo Frankly monologue (Part 1) in which Nate Hagens speculates how economic 'rules' flip from the upslope to the downslope of the fossil-fuel carbon pulse: efficiency becomes survival margin rather than Jevons rebound, lean/JIT gives way to buffers, proximity beats global scale (Hormuz as preview), labor and repair regain value, growth-dependent debt can't be made whole in real terms, distribution politics take center stage, and LLMs are biased toward the upslope rulebook.
Maximum power principle flips: efficiency becomes survival margin · 5Robustness over lean: buffers, slack and localization · 4Energy slaves get pay raises: labor and embodied energy revalued · 5Debt as a bet on a bigger future · 4Distribution politics and hyper-agents in a shrinking pie · 3AI trained on the upslope rulebook · 4
Maximum power principle flips: efficiency becomes survival margin 5
Nate Hagens argues the maximum power principle (Lotka, H.T. Odum) means winners capture the most available energy, but what that looks like depends on supply: in surplus it means fast indiscriminate growth, in scarcity it means squeezing the most work from a smaller flow.
framework🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: highmaximum power principleAlfred LotkaH.T. Odum
So what: Strategy regimes invert with energy availability; growth-at-all-costs business models (cash-burning startups) are upslope-phase strategies.
≈ “So not only has the winning strategy changed, but it's actually flipped.” paraphrase
Hagens claims Jevons paradox is not a law but the maximum power principle expressed during a pulse's growth phase; on the energy downslope, efficiency gains will deliver a net boost (savings fund essentials) rather than a rebound in consumption.
contrarian🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: mediumJevons paradoxmaximum power principle
So what: Challenges the standard assumption that efficiency gains always raise total energy demand; watch for demand destruction rather than rebound in a constrained-energy regime.
≈ “Jevons paradox was never really a law.” paraphrase
Three centuries of innovation in lighting technology never reduced global total human energy use for light, illustrating the Jevons rebound under growth conditions.
data_point🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: highJevons paradoxlighting
So what: Efficiency tech alone does not cut aggregate energy demand while the economy grows; be skeptical of efficiency-driven demand-decline forecasts.
Hagens says engineers focused on efficiency are not wrong but are at the wrong inflection point of the carbon pulse for efficiency to be the major driver today; it will become important on the downslope.
prediction🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: mediumcarbon pulseefficiency
So what: Efficiency-focused technologies may be early rather than wrong; their value proposition strengthens as net energy per capita declines.
Hagens predicts the efficient household, town or region on the downslope will be the one with margin for basic needs, as saved energy substitutes for the vanished societal surplus that used to cover heating and food.
prediction🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: mediumcarbon pulse
So what: Resilience of local economies may correlate with energy efficiency of essential services; relevant to municipal credit and regional real estate.
Robustness over lean: buffers, slack and localization 4
Hagens distinguishes two kinds of efficiency: more useful work per unit energy (to be rewarded on the downslope) versus 'running lean' by cutting buffers and reserves, which he expects to be punished on the downslope.
framework🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: highlean manufacturingjust-in-time
So what: Just-in-time, zero-inventory business models carry hidden fragility; favor firms with inventory, spare capacity and redundant suppliers.
≈ “This lean manufacturing, I think, is generally going to be punished” paraphrase
Hagens frames deliberately built slack — duplicate spare parts, stored food, backup suppliers and vendors — as insurance that may be the difference between an uncomfortable few weeks and calamity, echoing Olivier Hamant's 'robustness over efficiency'.
thesis🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: highOlivier Hamantsupply chains
So what: Expect rising corporate demand for redundancy, warehousing and supplier diversification; margins may compress as buffers are rebuilt.
≈ “build in slack and buffers deliberately” paraphrase
Globalized economies of scale and six-continent supply chains only work while the energy cost of moving goods is tiny; on the downslope every mile between production and use becomes both a cost and a vulnerability, especially for essential goods.
thesis🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: highglobalizationcontainer shippingsupply chains
So what: Supports reshoring/nearshoring and regional production of essentials; long-haul import-dependent models face structural headwinds.
≈ “closer will mean cheaper and will also mean safer” paraphrase
Hagens cites what is currently unfolding in Hormuz as a preview of the downslope rule that distance becomes a vulnerability for essential goods.
anecdote🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: mediumHormuz
So what: Strait of Hormuz disruption is a live test case of energy/shipping chokepoint risk; watch oil and shipping markets for persistence.
“What's unfolding now in Hormuz is, is” ▶ 16:40
Energy slaves get pay raises: labor and embodied energy revalued 5
Hagens restates that one barrel of oil does four to five years of human work, which made human and animal muscle nearly worthless as a source of work and led economies to engineer labor out as a cost.
data_point🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: highoil
So what: Labor-substitution economics rest on cheap oil; rising net energy cost changes the capital-vs-labor calculus.
Hagens predicts that as net energy per person falls, machines become costlier to run, fewer, or controlled by fewer people, so human and animal labor gradually re-enters the economy, first where fuel becomes scarcer and more expensive.
prediction🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: mediumoilautomation
So what: Watch fuel-scarce regions for early reversal of mechanization; real wages for manual labor could rise relative to energy-intensive capital.
≈ “when our energy slaves ask for significant pay raises” paraphrase
Hagens argues that on the downslope the energy already embodied in working possessions like furnaces and tractors becomes perhaps their most valuable attribute, so the rule flips from replace to repair and maintain.
thesis🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: mediumH.T. Odumembodied energy
So what: Favors repair, maintenance, refurbishment and spare-parts businesses over replacement-driven consumer durables.
Hagens expects most buildings that will be standing in the rich world in 2050 are already standing, even though current cultural incentives still favor new over repair.
prediction🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: mediumreal estatebuildings
So what: Bullish retrofit/renovation over new construction; existing building stock gains relative value.
Hagens expects households and communities that do many things reasonably well to outlast hyper-specialized ones that plan to buy everything else, as the complex supply system becomes less reliable.
prediction🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: mediumspecialization
So what: Generalist skills and local self-sufficiency gain value; dependence on complex service chains becomes a risk factor.
Debt as a bet on a bigger future 4
Hagens argues mortgages, pensions, sovereign bonds and the whole financial system are a bet that the future will be larger than the present, and since nearly all money is created as debt, money itself is the same bet.
thesis🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: highmortgagespensionssovereign bondsdebt
So what: Long-duration nominal claims carry systemic risk if real growth stalls; reassess duration exposure.
“our money itself is kind of the same bet” ▶ 19:16
Hagens claims that on the downslope, debt that assumes growth cannot be made whole in real terms whatever the nominal numbers say, because claims on the future become claims on something shrinking in aggregate; he says early signs of this phase shift are already in the news.
prediction🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: highdebtinflationsovereign bonds
So what: Implies real losses for bondholders via default, write-off or inflation; favors real assets over nominal claims.
≈ “debt that assumes growth cannot be made whole in real terms” paraphrase
Hagens's practical rule: avoid debt that only works if income keeps growing, and store savings in things that hold value when money does not, such as land, tools, soil, skills and relationships.
thesis🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: highlanddebt
So what: Explicit allocation tilt toward real assets (land, productive tools) and deleveraging.
Hagens expects a cultural return to the pre-carbon-pulse view that lending with interest is suspect and that unpayable debts invariably get defaulted on, written off or forgiven (jubilee).
prediction🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: mediumdebt jubileeinterest
So what: Political tail risk of debt forgiveness/restructuring; creditors face rising haircut risk.
“unpayable debts invariably get defaulted on” ▶ 21:07
Distribution politics and hyper-agents in a shrinking pie 3
Hagens argues growth did the hardest work of politics by making everyone's slice bigger; on the downslope, who gets what becomes the central political question.
causal🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: highpoliticsinequality
So what: Expect rising redistribution pressure, policy volatility and political risk premia as growth stalls.
Citing Joseph Tainter on collapse and Peter Turchin on elite overproduction, Hagens says societies that shared pain fairly held together through contraction while those where the top kept its slices came apart, so fairness becomes a metric of whether a country remains a country.
framework🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: mediumJoseph TainterPeter Turchin
So what: Inequality levels become a sovereign/political stability indicator in a low-growth world.
≈ “fairness is likely to stop being some nice slogan” paraphrase
Hagens predicts 'hyper agents' — the biggest corporations, the largest wealth holders and nation states — will resist the fairness rule change the longest because it is aimed at them.
prediction🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: mediumhyper agentscorporationsnation states
So what: Mega-caps and concentrated wealth face rising redistribution/tax/regulatory risk over time.
AI trained on the upslope rulebook 4
Hagens argues large language models were trained mostly on writing from the last and greatest century of the carbon pulse, so they encode the upslope rulebook and default to efficiency, scale, speed and growth when asked how to run a business, farm or nation.
contrarian🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: exploratoryAIlarge language models
So what: AI-driven strategy and forecasting may carry a systematic growth bias; treat AI outputs on long-term macro as consensus, not edge.
“AI is a consensus machine” ▶ 25:39
Hagens says downslope knowledge (H.T. Odum, Georgescu-Roegen, Limits to Growth) is in AI training data but buried under a billion pages assuming more is coming, making AI answers about the future 'unbelievably biased'.
thesis🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: exploratoryH.T. OdumGeorgescu-RoegenLimits to GrowthAI
So what: Contrarian analysis on resource limits is under-weighted by consensus tools — a potential source of edge.
Hagens speculates that updating AI models on the downslope would require a centralized mountain of energy, so models will either stay frozen in the upslope world or be retrained by and for the few who can afford them.
prediction🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: exploratoryAIlarge language models
So what: Frontier AI is energy-intensive and may concentrate among a few well-capitalized players as energy tightens.
Hagens argues knowledge that matters on the downslope — which seeds work locally, how to keep an old pump running — is local, oral and largely unwritten, so the human who knows things rather than the one who can search will matter again; a useful downslope AI would be small, local and solar-powered.
prediction🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: exploratoryAIsolar panels
So what: Possible niche for small, edge/offline, low-power AI; local expertise gains economic value.
“the human who knows things, rather than the&nbsp; human who can search, will start to matter again” ▶ 27:05

RealLifeLore 1 episode(s) · 21 insights

How the Cascadia Megaquake Will Change America Forever42 min21 insights2026-11-14
▶ Watch on YouTube
A geology explainer (not investment-focused) arguing the Cascadia Subduction Zone, not California's San Andreas, is America's deadliest quake risk: USGS puts a magnitude 9.0 full rupture at 10-15% in 50 years, and FEMA projects 13,000-25,000+ deaths, 1M+ displaced and tens to hundreds of billions in losses, with months-to-years utility outages across a largely pre-modern-code Pacific Northwest. The video ends with a Nebula sponsor segment.
Cascadia vs San Andreas: sizing the tail risk · 3Recurrence math and USGS probabilities · 4Building stock and infrastructure vulnerability · 5Tsunami, subsidence and the coastal death zone · 4Loss estimates and multi-year economic disruption · 3Preparedness gap and resilience spending · 2
Cascadia vs San Andreas: sizing the tail risk 3
The San Andreas fault's maximum earthquake is generally estimated at magnitude 8.0-8.2, whereas the Cascadia Subduction Zone can rupture its full ~620-mile length to produce a magnitude 9.0 or greater quake.
data_point🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: highSan Andreas faultCascadia Subduction Zone
So what: Catastrophe-risk models and insurers anchored on California may underweight the Pacific Northwest as the larger single-event US quake exposure.
“For the San Andreas, that upper range is generally estimated at around magnitude 8.0 to 8.2.” ▶ 1:24
A magnitude 9.0 Cascadia quake would release roughly 45 times the energy of the 1906 San Francisco earthquake and about 16 times that of a magnitude 8.2 San Andreas maximum event.
data_point🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: highCascadia Subduction ZoneSan Andreas fault
So what: Severity scaling is logarithmic; loss models should treat Cascadia as a different order of magnitude, not an incremental California-like event.
Cascadia's existence was only established around the early 1970s and its magnitude-9 capacity not widely understood until the late 1980s, so Pacific Northwest infrastructure was largely built without accounting for a catastrophic quake.
causal🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: highCascadia Subduction ZonePacific Northwest
So what: Hazard recognition lagged development by a century+, implying a large stock of underdesigned assets that the market and insurers may misprice.
Recurrence math and USGS probabilities 4
Chris Goldfinger's Oregon State sediment-core work identified about 41 major Cascadia earthquake events over 10,000 years, an average interval of ~243 years between major events and ~450-500 years between full-margin ruptures.
data_point🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: mediumChris GoldfingerOregon State UniversityCascadia Subduction Zone
So what: Recurrence intervals are the backbone of cat-bond pricing and reinsurance for the region; note scientists still debate attribution of some deposits.
More than 326 years have passed since the last full-margin rupture on 26 January 1700, though the host stresses this does not mean Cascadia is definitively 'overdue' since quakes are not on a schedule.
framework🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: mediumCascadia Subduction Zone
So what: Avoid 'overdue' narratives; use probabilistic hazard estimates rather than gambler's-fallacy timing when sizing exposure.
“that doesn't mean that the Cascadia subduction zone is definitively overdue since earthquakes don't really happen according to any predictable schedule” ▶ 13:53
The US Geological Survey estimates a 10-15% chance of a magnitude 9.0 full-margin Cascadia earthquake in the next 50 years, and about a 30% chance of magnitude 8.0+ on the southern section (northern California/southern Oregon) over the same period.
data_point🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: highUS Geological SurveyCascadia Subduction ZoneOregonCalifornia
So what: A ~1-in-8 half-century probability is material for 30-year mortgages, municipal bonds, utility assets and long-duration real estate in the region.
“a roughly 1 in8 chance of the big one, the magnitude 9.0 earthquake happening sometime within the next half century” ▶ 14:43
2025 research by Chris Goldfinger and colleagues found sediment evidence that several prehistoric Cascadia and northern San Andreas earthquakes occurred in close succession, raising the possibility that a Cascadia rupture could trigger a San Andreas quake.
contrarian🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: exploratoryChris GoldfingerCascadia Subduction ZoneSan Andreas faultCalifornia
So what: If confirmed, West Coast quake losses are correlated rather than independent, a key tail-dependence assumption for reinsurers and cat-bond portfolios.
“So far, the evidence remains uncertain” ▶ 33:22
Building stock and infrastructure vulnerability 5
Oregon only adopted statewide seismic design requirements in the 1970s and standards able to accommodate a magnitude 9.0 quake only in the 1990s, leaving many older buildings, bridges, schools, hospitals and fire stations vulnerable unless retrofitted.
data_point🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: highOregon
So what: Pre-1990s unretrofitted assets carry concentrated quake risk; retrofit demand is a multi-decade spending theme for engineering and construction firms.
Even modern seismic codes primarily aim to prevent collapse and protect life, not to keep structures undamaged, so newer buildings could still be economically lost after a Cascadia quake.
causal🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: highseismic codes
So what: Code compliance does not equal low insured loss; property owners and insurers should expect large damage even to modern stock.
“even modern seismic codes primarily aim to prevent catastrophic collapse and protect human life, rather than guaranteeing that structures will remain undamaged afterward” ▶ 15:48
Portland's roughly 6-mile lower Willamette River industrial stretch concentrates fuel storage, pipelines and critical energy infrastructure on liquefaction-prone soils, risking a major industrial and environmental disaster if tanks or pipelines fail.
causal🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: highPortlandWamtt Riverfuel storagepipelines
So what: Regional fuel supply is a single-point-of-failure; watch energy terminal operators' exposure and environmental liability in the Portland hub.
Seattle, Tacoma, Portland and the Willamette Valley sit on deep sedimentary basins and saturated soils that can amplify and prolong shaking and trigger liquefaction, while a full-margin rupture could shake for three to five minutes versus seconds for typical quakes.
causal🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: highSeattleTacomaPortlandWamid Valley
So what: Long-duration shaking plus basin amplification raises damage for tall buildings and buried utilities in the region's main metros.
Former FEMA regional administrator Kenneth Murphy described the agency's planning assumption as essentially everything west of Interstate 5 being 'toast'.
anecdote🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: highFEMAKenneth MurphyInterstate 5
So what: Official planners assume near-total loss west of I-5, a useful boundary for mapping concentrated exposure.
“essentially everything west of Interstate 5 would be toast” ▶ 21:05
Tsunami, subsidence and the coastal death zone 4
When Cascadia ruptures, parts of the Oregon and Washington coast could permanently sink up to about 2 m (6.5 ft) within minutes, and 2025 research found this subsidence can more than double the people, buildings and roads exposed to coastal flooding under some scenarios.
data_point🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: mediumOregonWashington
So what: Post-quake land becomes permanently flood-exposed, compounding sea-level-rise risk for coastal real estate, roads and farmland valuations.
First tsunami waves could reach the coast within 10-20 minutes, with run-up of several tens of feet and possibly over 100 ft in exposed spots, and road evacuation will be largely impossible.
data_point🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: highCascadia Subduction Zonetsunami
So what: Coastal hospitality and tourism assets face near-total loss potential with little warning; ShakeAlert gives only seconds to tens of seconds.
Washington's Long Beach Peninsula, averaging 10-15 ft elevation with one road to the mainland, 11,000 residents and tens of thousands of summer tourists, is described as a geographic death trap; a 2025 Washington state assessment estimated 18,000-25,000 casualties in Pacific County alone in a summer scenario.
data_point🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: highLong Beach PeninsulaPacific CountyWashington
So what: Hyper-local concentration of life and property risk; a key example for insurers and municipal-credit analysts of uninsurable coastal exposure.
“the Long Beach Peninsula here is a complete geographic death trap” ▶ 29:31
Roughly one in three Oregon coast residents are 65 or older, double the national average, worsening on-foot tsunami evacuation outcomes.
data_point🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: mediumOregon
So what: Demographics amplify casualty estimates and post-disaster healthcare and relocation costs for the coastal economy.
Loss estimates and multi-year economic disruption 3
FEMA projects a full Cascadia event could kill 13,000 to more than 25,000 people, injure 27,000-107,000, and displace more than a million, with economic costs likely in the tens to hundreds of billions of dollars.
data_point🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: highFEMA
So what: This would be the largest natural disaster in US history, implying federal fiscal strain, reinsurance capital hits and regional credit stress.
“The total economic cost of the disaster will be anybody's best guess. But it will almost certainly run up into the tens, if not the hundreds of billions of dollars.” ▶ 30:43
Oregon's resilience planning report projects Willamette Valley power outages up to 3 months, coastal outages up to 6 months, and water and sewer restoration taking up to a year in the valley and three years on the coast.
data_point🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: highOregonWamid ValleyPortlandSalemEugene
So what: Prolonged utility outages imply extended business interruption losses and heavy capex for regional utilities after the event.
A full-margin rupture would hit multiple metros at once, disrupting the ports, highways, factories and technology industries of Seattle and Portland, while the Cascade Mountains isolate the region from outside aid.
causal🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: mediumSeattlePortlandCascade Mountains
So what: Supply chains reliant on PNW ports and tech operations face correlated, prolonged disruption; geographic isolation slows recovery.
Preparedness gap and resilience spending 2
The region now has earthquake early warning (ShakeAlert, statewide in Oregon and Washington since 2021), stronger codes, tsunami signage, drills and some vertical evacuation structures, but fully retrofitting pre-1970s/80s buildings and bridges will take decades of sustained investment.
thesis🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: highShake AlertOregonWashington
So what: Seismic retrofit and resilience infrastructure is a long-duration public spending pipeline worth tracking in state and federal budgets.
Every year without a major quake makes it easier to postpone expensive preparation, creating a behavioral underinvestment problem.
causal🗣 host (RealLifeLore narrator)conviction: highPacific Northwest
So what: Complacency suggests resilience spending and insurance take-up stay below risk-justified levels until a catalyst event.
“every year that passes without a major earthquake happening just makes it easier for people to postpone the expensive preparations” ▶ 34:41
